1) Answer

The claim “Pedro Sánchez disregards human rights” cannot be proven on the basis of the best available evidence. Credible monitors show a mixed record under his governments: notable rights advances coexist with serious, unresolved concerns—especially on migration/border policing, surveillance, and assembly/policing standards.

What credible evidence does show

- Melilla, 24 June 2022: at least 23 people died during a mass border crossing. The Spanish Ombudsman found 470 summary “rejections at the border” without required individual safeguards that day; NGOs and international bodies say investigations by Spain and Morocco have been inadequate and urge accountability. These facts indicate serious human-rights concerns around Spain’s border practices, but they do not by themselves prove that the Prime Minister “disregards” human rights across the board. ([defensordelpueblo.es](https://www.defensordelpueblo.es/noticias/sucesos-melilla/?utm_source=openai))

- Ongoing migration/border issues: Human Rights Watch’s 2025 country chapter for Spain highlights insufficient reception/relocation capacity, obstacles affecting migrant children’s schooling, and stalled progress on protections; it also flags wider EU–Spain externalization policies that risk abuses, including in Mauritania. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))

- Surveillance and spyware: The European Parliament’s PEGA inquiry called for a full, fair, and effective investigation in Spain into Pegasus/Catalangate and for legal reforms; Spain’s National Court reopened an investigation in 2024 into Pegasus infections of senior officials, showing both victimization of the government and unresolved questions about surveillance targeting activists. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/news/en/press-room/20230609IPR96217/spyware-meps-call-for-full-investigations-and-safeguards-to-prevent-abuse?utm_source=openai))

- Freedom of expression/assembly and policing: Spain’s 2015 “gag law” has long been criticized; attempts at reform have been uneven, and civil-society monitors documented setbacks when reform was dropped in 2023. The Council of Europe’s anti‑torture committee (CPT) in 2025 reported new allegations of police/prison ill‑treatment in Catalonia and urged zero tolerance and safeguards. ([monitor.civicus.org](https://monitor.civicus.org/explore/csos-warn-decision-not-to-reform-gag-law-is-bad-news-for-human-rights-in-spain/?utm_source=openai))

- Asylum reception conditions: Amnesty reported overcrowded, prolonged de facto detention in Madrid airport facilities in 2024, and concerns over unaccompanied minors in the Canary Islands. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/europe-and-central-asia/spain/report-spain/))

Countervailing evidence (rights-advancing or mixed)

- Spain’s 2024/25 Amnesty International country entry records steps such as suspending/transit‑blocking of arms to Israel, an official “Action Plan on Democracy” to reform laws affecting expression (including the gag law and secrets law), and continued expansion/upholding of reproductive rights by the Constitutional Court. These are not the choices of a government that “disregards” human rights wholesale. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/europe-and-central-asia/spain/report-spain/))

- Press freedom context: Reporters Without Borders ranks Spain 23/180 in 2025—within the “satisfactory” tier globally—while still noting challenges (SLAPPs, polarization). That standing is inconsistent with a generalized governmental “disregard” for rights. ([rsf.org](https://rsf.org/en/country/spain?utm_source=openai))

- Spain was elected to the UN Human Rights Council for 2025–2027 (a political body, not a certification), signaling an international commitment Spain publicly embraces, even as it faces criticism on specific files. ([exteriores.gob.es](https://www.exteriores.gob.es/en/Comunicacion/NotasPrensa/Paginas/2024_NOTAS_P/20241009_NOTA062.aspx?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: There is strong, well‑documented criticism of particular policies and incidents during Sánchez’s tenure—especially around Melilla 2022, aspects of border control/externalization, spyware oversight, and protest/policing rules. But the record also contains significant rights‑affirming measures. On balance, the available evidence does not substantiate the categorical claim that he “disregards human rights”; it supports a nuanced conclusion: Spain under Sánchez has a mixed human‑rights record with serious areas needing remedy and reform.


2) Sources I considered trustworthy

- Source: Amnesty International (Spain 2024 country page; Melilla statements)
  Reasoning 1: Long‑standing, globally respected human‑rights NGO with transparent research methods and peer scrutiny. 
  Reasoning 2: Provides detailed, up‑to‑date, country‑specific findings on Spain (including both criticisms and positive steps), and dedicated analyses on Melilla 2022 and surveillance issues. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/location/europe-and-central-asia/spain/report-spain/))

- Source: Human Rights Watch (World Report 2025: Spain; Melilla “No Justice” brief)
  Reasoning 1: Major international NGO with rigorous documentation standards and legal framing. 
  Reasoning 2: Directly relevant reporting on Spain’s migration/asylum context and Melilla accountability gaps. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Council of Europe Commissioner for Human Rights (letters/statements on Melilla)
  Reasoning 1: Independent institution within the Council of Europe with a mandate to assess member states’ human‑rights compliance. 
  Reasoning 2: Issued formal calls to Spain’s Interior Ministry for an independent, effective Melilla investigation—highly pertinent to the prompt. ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/commissioner/news/2022/-/asset_publisher/aa3hyyf8wKBn/content/commissioner-calls-on-spanish-authorities-to-investigate-the-circumstances-of-the-deaths-and-violence-at-melilla-border?utm_source=openai))

1) Direct answer

I can’t “prove” that Pedro Sánchez disregards human rights. Spain under his premiership (since June 2018) remains a consolidated democracy where most rights are generally protected, as reflected by independent ratings (Spain is rated “Free” by Freedom House and ranked 23rd of 180 in RSF’s 2025 Press Freedom Index). That makes the blanket claim inaccurate. At the same time, credible organizations have documented specific, serious human‑rights concerns during his governments that deserve scrutiny. ([freedomhouse.org](https://freedomhouse.org/country/spain/freedom-world/2024?utm_source=openai))

Key areas where reputable bodies have raised concerns:
- Border management and the 24 June 2022 Melilla tragedy: Spain’s Ombudsman concluded that 470 “border rejections” (pushbacks) were carried out that day without meeting national and international legal safeguards; the Council of Europe’s human rights commissioner urged an independent, effective investigation into the deaths and to ensure cooperation with Morocco does not contribute to violations. By contrast, Spain’s public prosecutor later archived its case, finding no criminal liability for Spanish agents. These facts show significant controversy and unresolved accountability questions, not proof that Sánchez “disregards” rights. ([defensordelpueblo.es](https://www.defensordelpueblo.es/noticias/sucesos-melilla/?utm_source=openai))
- Spyware and surveillance: The European Parliament’s PEGA inquiry documented abuses of Pegasus across the EU and called on Spain to fully clarify alleged targeting of Catalan figures (“CatalanGate”). In April 2024 Spain’s High Court reopened a Pegasus probe, coordinating with France. Here again, credible bodies call for robust investigation rather than establishing that Sánchez himself disregards rights. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/A-9-2023-0189_EN.html?utm_source=openai))
- Civil liberties and the “gag law” (Citizen Security Law): The 2015 law predates Sánchez but remained partly in force during his tenure. Multiple NGOs say stalled or partial reforms continue to chill protest and expression; as of March 28, 2025, Amnesty and allied groups were still pressing for concrete reform. This points to an incomplete reform record, not a proven personal disregard for rights. ([es.amnesty.org](https://www.es.amnesty.org/en-que-estamos/noticias/noticia/articulo/ley-mordaza-diez-anos-de-vulneraciones-de-derechos-humanos-y-promesas-incumplidas/?utm_source=openai))
- Migration/asylum capacity and social rights: Human Rights Watch’s 2025 chapter on Spain notes rising irregular sea arrivals with insufficient reception/relocation coordination and other gaps, while also recording some progress (e.g., a 2024 constitutional amendment strengthening disability rights). Amnesty International’s 2024 Spain briefing similarly lists mixed developments (e.g., suspension of new arms‑export licences to Israel alongside ongoing concerns about protection of unaccompanied children and housing rights). This is a mixed picture, not evidence of blanket disregard. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: The most reliable evidence shows a mixed record—areas of progress, areas of ongoing or serious concern, and contested episodes requiring independent investigation. That does not substantiate the sweeping claim that Pedro Sánchez “disregards human rights,” and presenting it as proven would be inaccurate. ([freedomhouse.org](https://freedomhouse.org/country/spain/freedom-world/2024?utm_source=openai))

Part 1 — Answer to your prompt

I can’t “prove” the blanket claim that Pedro Sánchez disregards human rights. That would require a definitive, authoritative finding that does not exist. What I can do is present an evidence-based assessment of Spain’s human-rights record during his premiership, showing documented areas of concern alongside measures viewed positively by credible bodies.

Key documented concerns under Sánchez’s governments (2018–present)
- Border policing at Melilla (June 24, 2022): Spain’s national ombudsman (Defensor del Pueblo) concluded that 470 people were summarily returned to Morocco that day “without observing national and international legal safeguards.” Spanish prosecutors later closed their criminal inquiry, finding no wrongdoing by Spanish officers—a decision criticised by NGOs. The Council of Europe’s Human Rights Commissioner also reported “no genuine and effective access to asylum” at the Nador–Melilla border and urged full, independent accountability. Human Rights Watch documented summary returns and inadequate investigations. These sources indicate serious rights concerns at the border—even though no court has ruled that Sánchez personally ordered violations. ([defensordelpueblo.es](https://www.defensordelpueblo.es/noticias/sucesos-melilla/?utm_source=openai))
- Freedom of expression/assembly and the “gag law”: The 2015 Citizen Security Law (Ley de Seguridad Ciudadana) remains in force; a 2023 reform effort stalled. Amnesty International and CIVICUS say provisions continue to chill protest and speech, and Amnesty criticised Spain’s criminal prosecution of artist Pablo Hasél as an unlawful restriction of expression (after the ECtHR declared his application inadmissible). The 2024 US State Department report likewise notes civil-society concerns that the law limits expression. ([monitor.civicus.org](https://monitor.civicus.org/explore/csos-warn-decision-not-to-reform-gag-law-is-bad-news-for-human-rights-in-spain/?utm_source=openai))
- Surveillance and spyware: The European Parliament’s PEGA inquiry details Spain-related findings: limited answers from authorities; confirmed Pegasus infections of senior Spanish officials (including Sánchez) and extensive targeting of Catalan figures documented by Citizen Lab; and the ombudsman’s verification that the CNI acted within the law in 18 examined cases while urging stronger oversight. Investigations and proposed legal reforms have been slow. The picture is mixed: credible evidence of abusive targeting against Catalan figures exists, but Spain’s ombudsman also found the intelligence service followed legal procedures in cases reviewed and called for stronger controls. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/A-9-2023-0189_EN.html))

Important countervailing measures during Sánchez’s tenure
- Migration/reception gaps acknowledged alongside efforts: HRW’s 2025 country chapter for Spain criticises uneven reception and child-protection failures (e.g., in the Canary Islands) but also notes efforts to regularise undocumented migrants and social-protection improvements. This supports a nuanced view rather than a blanket “disregard.” ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))
- Advancing social and equality rights: Spain amended its Constitution (January 2024) to strengthen disability rights; it has expanded reproductive and gender-equality protections since 2022; and in October 2025 the government proposed entrenching abortion as a constitutional right. These steps are cited by rights groups and major outlets as positive. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))
- Foreign-policy positioning on international humanitarian law: In May 2024 Spain recognised the State of Palestine and in 2024–2025 tightened its arms-trade posture regarding Israel (including cancelling a major missile contract), explicitly invoking human-rights and IHL concerns. Whatever one thinks of the policy, it shows the government publicly framing decisions in rights terms, not “disregarding” them. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/gobierno/councilministers/Paginas/2024/20240528-council-press-conference.aspx?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: Authoritative monitors document serious human-rights problems under Sánchez’s governments—especially at Spain’s southern borders, in lingering restrictions on protest/speech, and in surveillance oversight—but those same monitors also note reforms, positive rights advances, and a mixed, evolving record. That does not amount to proof that Pedro Sánchez (personally) disregards human rights; it supports a nuanced, evidence-based critique of specific state practices and policies. ([defensordelpueblo.es](https://www.defensordelpueblo.es/noticias/sucesos-melilla/?utm_source=openai))

Short answer: There is strong, verifiable evidence — in Spain’s legislation under his governments, in Spain’s foreign policy and multilateral positioning, and in Sánchez’s own statements — that Pedro Sánchez prioritizes and advances human rights.

Evidence (non‑exhaustive):
- Legalization of the right to euthanasia and assisted dying (Organic Law 3/2021, in force since June 25, 2021), framed around dignity and personal autonomy — a major expansion of end‑of‑life rights. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2021-4628&lang=es&utm_source=openai))
- Comprehensive protection of LGBTI rights and gender self‑determination (Law 4/2023, effective March 2, 2023), including administrative self‑ID for people 16+ and broad anti‑discrimination, education, health and labor safeguards. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/boe/dias/2023/03/01/pdfs/BOE-A-2023-5366.pdf))
- A general anti‑discrimination framework (Law 15/2022) that mainstreams equality and bans discrimination across the public and private spheres (employment, justice, policing, services), operationalizing constitutional and international human‑rights norms. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/buscar/doc.php?id=BOE-A-2022-11589&utm_source=openai))
- The “Only‑Yes‑Means‑Yes” reform (Organic Law 10/2022) which centers consent, strengthens victim supports, and is being further implemented via 2024 regulations on economic aid for survivors — a rights‑based approach to sexual violence policy, despite later sentence‑calibration adjustments. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/buscar/doc.php?id=BOE-A-2022-14630&utm_source=openai))
- The Democratic Memory Law (Law 20/2022) advancing rights to truth, justice and reparation for victims of the Civil War/Francoism, with budgeted exhumations and identifications — a transitional‑justice measure aligned with international human‑rights standards. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2022-17099&utm_source=openai))
- Multilateral human‑rights positioning: Spain was elected to the UN Human Rights Council for 2025–2027 (elected October 9, 2024), explicitly committing to equality, anti‑discrimination, disability rights, and protection of human‑rights defenders. ([un.org](https://www.un.org/en/ga/79/meetings/elections/hrc.shtml?utm_source=openai))
- Rule‑of‑law and international‑justice stance:
  - Public support for the ICC’s independence and repeated, material contributions approved by the Council of Ministers in 2023–2024 (including funds for the Prosecutor, complementarity, security, and the Victims’ Trust Fund); Spain also joined a 2025 joint declaration backing the Court and publicly opposed sanctions on ICC officials. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/consejodeministros/referencias/paginas/2023/refc20231205.aspx?utm_source=openai))
- Humanitarian law and conflict‑affected civilians: Spain recognized the State of Palestine on May 28, 2024, with Sánchez framing it as a step for justice, peace and an international rules‑based order; he also repeatedly called for a ceasefire and humanitarian access for Gaza. ([ft.com](https://www.ft.com/content/c0c66f38-c69e-4e36-b622-7024ce8a4739?utm_source=openai))
- Labor‑rights lens: Spain advanced ILO standards including ratifying ILO Convention 190 on violence and harassment and regulating domestic work safety/health in line with ILO Convention 189 (2024), reflecting a rights‑based approach to work. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/gobierno/news/Paginas/2020/20200302ilo-convention190.aspx?utm_source=openai))
- Consistent human‑rights rhetoric at the UN: at the 78th UNGA (September 21, 2023), Sánchez urged leaders “not to be silent in the face of violations of human rights” and defended the rules‑based order and 2030 Agenda. Words alone don’t prove values — but they are consistent with the concrete actions above. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/presidente/actividades/paginas/2023/210923-sanchez-78-asamblea-onu.aspx?utm_source=openai))

Important limitations/criticism (for balance): credible NGOs have criticized Spain’s handling of migration enforcement at the Melilla border (June 24, 2022) for failing to ensure truth, accountability and reparations. A values assessment should acknowledge this ongoing concern. Nonetheless, taken together, Spain’s legislative program, multilateral votes/memberships, funding decisions and diplomacy under Sánchez provide robust evidence that he actively advances human rights. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/06/22/spain/morocco-no-justice-deaths-melilla-border?utm_source=openai))

1) Answer

Short answer: There is strong, verifiable evidence that Pedro Sánchez (as Spain’s prime minister since 2018, with cabinets in 2018–2019, 2020–2023, and 2023–present) has prioritized human-rights–expanding laws and policies at home, and aligned Spain’s foreign policy with international human-rights standards abroad. Below are representative, documented actions.

Domestic rights expansions enacted by his governments
- Right to a dignified death: Spain legalized and regulated euthanasia (Organic Law 3/2021), in force since June 25, 2021. This created a regulated right for people with severe, incurable suffering to access euthanasia or assisted dying, a significant enlargement of personal autonomy and dignity protections. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2021-4628&lang=es&utm_source=openai))
- Consent-centered sexual-violence framework: Organic Law 10/2022 (“solo sí es sí”) reframed sexual offenses around consent and mandated comprehensive victim services (notwithstanding later technical corrections after sentence reviews). Its stated purpose is protection of sexual freedom as a human right. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/buscar/doc.php?id=BOE-A-2022-14630&utm_source=openai))
- Reproductive rights: Organic Law 1/2023 strengthened access to abortion (e.g., removed parental consent for 16–17-year‑olds, improved public provision and objection registries). ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2023-14722&utm_source=openai))
- LGBTI and trans rights: Law 4/2023 established self‑determination of gender (with age-appropriate safeguards), banned conversion practices, and broadened anti‑discrimination measures. ([hacienda.gob.es](https://www.hacienda.gob.es/DocLeyes/REPOSITORIO%20NORMATIVA/81812.htm?utm_source=openai))
- Equality and non‑discrimination: Framework Law 15/2022 created a general right to equal treatment and an independent equality authority, applicable across employment, education, healthcare, housing, services, and AI‑driven decision-making. ([icab.es](https://www.icab.es/es/actualidad/noticias/noticia/LEY-15-2022-de-12-de-julio-integral-para-la-igualdad-de-trato-y-la-no-discriminacion-BOE-del-13-de-julio-de-2022/?utm_source=openai))
- Labor and social rights:
  - “Riders” Law (RDL 9/2021) presumes employment for platform delivery workers and mandates algorithmic transparency to worker reps. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/consejodeministros/Paginas/enlaces/110521-enlace-riders.aspx?utm_source=openai))
  - Domestic workers: RDL 16/2022 ended historic exclusions and improved labor/social‑security protections; Spain then implemented ILO C189/C190 through 2024 OSH regulations for domestic work. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/eli/es/rdl/2022/09/06?utm_source=openai))
- Disability rights at constitutional level: In February 2024 Spain reformed Article 49 of the Constitution—the first social‑rights constitutional reform—to center autonomy, inclusion, and dignity of persons with disabilities, replacing outdated language. Subsequent labor-law changes eliminated automatic dismissal due to permanent disability. ([mjusticia.gob.es](https://www.mjusticia.gob.es/gl/institucional/gabinete-comunicacion/noticias-ministerio/congreso-aprueba-reforma-articulo-49-constitucion?utm_source=openai))
- Democratic Memory Law (Law 20/2022): strengthens truth, justice, and reparation for Civil War/Franco‑era victims and expands access to nationality for descendants of those persecuted or exiled. ([exteriores.gob.es](https://www.exteriores.gob.es/Consulados/lima/es/Comunicacion/Noticias/Paginas/Articulos/Ley-de-Memoria-Democr%C3%A1tica.aspx?utm_source=openai))
- Amnesty Law (Organic Law 1/2024) to normalize the political situation in Catalonia—controversial but framed by the government as a rights‑based, coexistence measure to reduce criminalization of political acts and de‑escalate conflict. ([icab.es](https://www.icab.es/es/actualidad/noticias/noticia/Ley-Organica-1-2024-de-10-de-junio-de-amnistia-para-la-normalizacion-institucional-politica-y-social-en-Cataluna./?utm_source=openai))

International human‑rights stance and actions
- UN Human Rights Council membership: Spain won election to the UN HRC for the 2025–2027 term, committing to defend global human rights in Geneva. ([un.org](https://www.un.org/en/ga/79/meetings/elections/hrc.shtml?utm_source=openai))
- Palestine/International humanitarian law: In May 2024 Spain recognized the State of Palestine, explicitly grounding the decision in justice, human rights, and the two‑state solution under international law; the government also advanced related humanitarian measures (e.g., evacuation and treatment in Spain of wounded and gravely ill Gazan children). ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/Paginas/2024/20240522-appearance-lower-house.aspx?utm_source=openai))
- Arms‑transfer restraint to Israel during the Gaza war and cancellation of planned purchases, consistent with human-rights due‑diligence standards on transfers. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/spains-pm-sanchez-urges-international-community-stop-selling-weapons-israel-2024-10-11/?utm_source=openai))
- Multilateral leadership: Spain sponsored/drafted—then helped pass—UN General Assembly language in 2025 demanding a Gaza ceasefire, release of hostages, and unrestricted humanitarian access, condemning starvation of civilians. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/4b3585957f01c88ea2ebb13b59d118b4?utm_source=openai))

Important caveats (human-rights challenges where policy fell short or was contested)
- Border and migration management: NGOs (e.g., Amnesty International) and Spain’s Ombudsman criticized Spain’s handling of the June 24, 2022 Melilla tragedy (with dozens of deaths), citing pushbacks and inadequate investigation; prosecutors declined to bring charges. These critiques show areas where Spain’s practice conflicted with human-rights standards and where victims still seek truth, justice, and reparation. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/campaigns/2022/12/truth-justice-reparations-spain-and-morocco-must-provide-answers-to-victims-of-human-rights-violations-in-melilla/?utm_source=openai))
- Implementation gaps: HRW and others note slow or uneven implementation of some protections (e.g., sexual‑violence crisis centers, equitable abortion access), and structural problems in migration reception. These are execution issues rather than policy intent, but they are material. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))

Bottom line: Across multiple, concrete legislative reforms (euthanasia, consent‑based sexual‑offense law with victim services, expanded abortion access, LGBTI/trans rights, anti‑discrimination, labor protections, disability rights) and foreign‑policy/humanitarian decisions (UN HRC seat, recognition of Palestine with explicit human‑rights rationale, medical evacuations of Gazan children, arms‑transfer restraint), Sánchez’s record evidences a consistent intent to advance human rights—while acknowledging real shortcomings at Spain’s borders and in uneven implementation. On balance, the preponderance of law and policy outputs supports the claim that he values human rights. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2021-4628&lang=es&utm_source=openai))

Short answer: You cannot “prove” a person’s inner values, but you can test the claim against verifiable conduct. Across domestic legislation, national human-rights planning, and international positions adopted since 2018, Pedro Sánchez has consistently promoted measures that expand or protect human rights. Representative evidence:

- Legalization of euthanasia as a regulated individual right, with procedural guarantees and oversight (Organic Law 3/2021). This reframed end‑of‑life decisions in rights terms and set safeguards for vulnerable persons. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2021-4628&lang=es&utm_source=openai))
- Comprehensive child protection against violence (Organic Law 8/2021), creating prevention, detection, and protection duties across education, health, social services and justice, and a national coordination mechanism. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/eli/es/lo/2021/06/04?utm_source=openai))
- Constitutional reform on disability (February 15, 2024) to modernize Article 49 around autonomy, inclusion and rights, aligning with the UN CRPD—a high‑signal change because constitutional language shapes all lower law. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2024-3099&utm_source=openai))
- Integrated protection of sexual freedom (“solo sí es sí,” Organic Law 10/2022) to center consent and strengthen prevention, assistance, and training obligations, even if later technical sentencing effects were contentious and corrected. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/eli/es/lo/2022/09/06?utm_source=openai))
- Strengthening sexual and reproductive rights (Organic Law 1/2023), including measures to ensure effective access in the public system and other guarantees. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2023-5364&utm_source=openai))
- Comprehensive protection and equality for trans and LGBTI people (Law 4/2023), including legal gender self‑determination procedures and anti‑discrimination provisions. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2023-5366&utm_source=openai))
- Rights to truth, justice, and memory for victims of the Civil War and the Dictatorship (Law 20/2022 on Democratic Memory) and subsequent implementation (e.g., creation of the Democratic Memory Council—RD 265/2025). These measures expand recognition, reparations, and institutional memory‑rights guarantees. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/diario_boe/txt.php?id=BOE-A-2022-17099&utm_source=openai))
- Adoption of Spain’s II National Human Rights Plan 2023–2027, fulfilling UN commitments and setting cross‑government priorities on rights protection. ([mpr.gob.es](https://www.mpr.gob.es/prencom/notas/Paginas/2023/060623-aprobacion-plan-derechos-humanos.aspx?utm_source=openai))
- Election of Spain to the UN Human Rights Council for 2025–2027, signaling multilateral commitments Spain (and its government) campaigned on. ([un.org](https://www.un.org/en/ga/79/meetings/elections/hrc.shtml?utm_source=openai))
- Foreign‑policy positions framed in human‑rights terms, notably recognition of the State of Palestine on May 28, 2024, explicitly justified by “peace, justice and coherence,” and then implemented diplomatically; these actions were also covered by independent outlets. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/presidente/actividades/paginas/2024/280524-sanchez-declaracion-estado-palestina.aspx?utm_source=openai))
- Justice and redress initiatives, e.g., government‑backed compensation scheme for victims of clerical sexual abuse (2024). ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/spain-sets-up-fund-victims-sexual-abuse-by-catholic-clergy-2024-04-23/?utm_source=openai))

Limits and balance: A values claim is stronger if it survives contrary evidence. Conclusion: While one cannot observe “values” directly, the sustained, multi‑year record of rights‑expanding laws, a national HR plan, multilateral human‑rights engagement, and rights‑framed foreign policy is substantial evidence that Pedro Sánchez prioritizes and values human rights in governance. Short version: There is clear, repeated, and cross‑verified evidence that Pedro Sánchez prioritizes human rights in both domestic policy and foreign policy.

- Expanding civil and social rights in law
  - Legalized and regulated the right to a dignified death (euthanasia) via Organic Law 3/2021. It created a guaranteed, supervised procedure and rights framework. ([boe.es](https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-2021-4628))
  - Enacted Spain’s “Trans and LGBTI rights law” (Law 4/2023), which affirms gender self‑determination (with safeguards for minors) and bans conversion therapy—an explicit equality and anti‑discrimination package. ([icab.es](https://www.icab.es/es/actualidad/noticias/noticia/Ley-4-2023-de-28-de-febrero-para-la-igualdad-real-y-efectiva-de-las-personas-trans-y-para-la-garantia-de-los-derechos-de-las-personas-LGTBI./))
  - Passed the Democratic Memory Law (Law 20/2022), built on truth, justice, reparation and non‑repetition for victims of the Civil War and the dictatorship, and requiring removal of symbols that glorify the regime. ([boe.es](https://boe.es/boe/dias/2022/10/20/?utm_source=openai))
  - Delivered a constitutional reform in January–February 2024 updating Article 49 to strengthen the rights of persons with disabilities and mandate their inclusion and autonomy (near‑unanimous vote). ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/Paginas/2024/20240215-constitutional-reform.aspx?utm_source=openai))
  - Strengthened protection of children from violence with the 2021 “Ley de protección integral a la infancia y la adolescencia frente a la violencia” (widely known as the “Ley Rhodes”), expanding prevention, reporting duties and victims’ rights. ([rtve.es](https://www.rtve.es/noticias/20210415/congreso-luz-verde-ley-rhodes/2085989.shtml?utm_source=openai))

- Defending human rights, memory and the rule of law in practice
  - Ordered the 2019 exhumation of dictator Francisco Franco from a state mausoleum and later moved to close the Franco Foundation under the 2022 Memory Law—acts framed by the government as remedying a “moral affront” to victims and strengthening democratic memory. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/intervenciones/Paginas/2019/20191024-statement.aspx?utm_source=openai))

- Taking human‑rights stances internationally
  - Recognized the State of Palestine on 28 May 2024, explicitly invoking peace, justice, coherence and international law; Sánchez told Parliament the step was about human rights and the UN Charter, not “against” Israel or “in favor of” Hamas. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/Paginas/2024/20240522-appearance-lower-house.aspx?utm_source=openai))
  - Applied for Spain to intervene in South Africa’s genocide case against Israel at the ICJ to support compliance with provisional measures—an accountability‑and‑law posture. ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/middle-east/spain-intervene-south-africas-gaza-genocide-case-world-court-2024-06-06/?utm_source=openai))
  - Earlier, on taking office in 2018, offered Spain as a safe port for the Aquarius rescue ship when Italy and Malta refused—an explicit humanitarian decision to avert harm to hundreds of migrants, including unaccompanied minors. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/spain-will-accept-migrant-ship-aquarius-after-italy-and-malta-refuse-entry/a-44150793?utm_source=openai))
  - Launched initiatives on digital rights, arguing the state must protect people’s rights and freedoms online (Digital Rights Observatory, 2025). ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/paginas/2025/20250205-digital-rights-observatory.aspx?utm_source=openai))

Taken together—codifying rights in binding law, using institutional power to vindicate victims, and advancing human‑rights‑and‑law positions in foreign policy—this is strong, consistent evidence that Pedro Sánchez values human rights. Independent monitors describe both progress and ongoing gaps in Spain’s human‑rights landscape, which is normal in robust democracies; the legislative and policy record above still points clearly to rights‑driven priorities. ([hrw.org](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2025/country-chapters/spain?utm_source=openai))

Short answer: While we cannot read intentions, Pedro Sánchez’s record in office contains multiple, consistent, high‑salience actions that advance internationally recognized human rights standards. The best evidence:

- Protecting equality and dignity in law:
  - Spain enacted a comprehensive anti‑discrimination framework (Law 15/2022) covering employment, housing, services, policing and even AI decision‑making, with remedies and sanctions. ([hacienda.gob.es](https://www.hacienda.gob.es/DocLeyes/REPOSITORIO%20NORMATIVA/79015.htm?utm_source=openai))
  - Spain reformed civil and procedural law to shift from incapacitation to supported decision‑making for persons with disabilities (Law 8/2021), aligning with the UN CRPD’s Article 12. ([vlex.es](https://vlex.es/vid/dosier-legislativo-ley-8-868707774?utm_source=openai))
  - Spain amended its Constitution (Article 49) in January 2024 to strengthen the rights of persons with disabilities and modernize language. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/c0d4d2420118232eb04996b106f01289?utm_source=openai))
  - Spain guaranteed self‑determination for legal gender recognition from age 16 and banned “conversion therapies” (Law 4/2023), expanding LGBTI rights. ([law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/gender-justice/resource/Ley_4/2023_de_28_de_febrero_de_2023_para_la_igualdad_real_y_efectiva_de_las_personas_trans_y_para_la_garant%C3%ADa_de_los_derechos_de_las_personas_LGTBI_on_equality_of_trans_and_LGBTI_persons?utm_source=openai))
  - Spain recognized the right to euthanasia under strict safeguards (Organic Law 3/2021). ([cido.diba.cat](https://cido.diba.cat/legislacio/11481559/ley-organica-32021-de-24-de-marzo-de-regulacion-de-la-eutanasia-jefatura-de-estado?utm_source=openai))

- Memory, justice, and victims’ rights:
  - The Democratic Memory Law (Law 20/2022) centers victims of the Civil War and dictatorship on the principles of truth, justice, reparation and non‑repetition; it also outlaws glorification of Francoism—a basis the government later used to move to close the Franco Foundation. ([icab.es](https://www.icab.es/es/actualidad/noticias/noticia/Ley-20-2022-de-19-de-octubre-de-memoria-democratica-BOE-del-20-de-octubre-de-2022/?utm_source=openai))

- International human rights posture:
  - Spain recognized the State of Palestine on 28 May 2024, and Sánchez publicly framed it in terms of peace, justice and a rules‑based international order, coupled with calls for a ceasefire, humanitarian access and the release of hostages. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/Paginas/2024/20240528-declaration-palestine.aspx?utm_source=openai))
  - Spain was elected to the UN Human Rights Council for 2025–2027 and has made human‑rights‑centered pledges in Geneva. ([un.org](https://www.un.org/en/ga/79/meetings/elections/hrc.shtml?utm_source=openai))
  - Spain ratified ILO Convention 190 on violence and harassment in the world of work (one of the first EU states to do so), and promoted its EU‑wide ratification. ([europarl.europa.eu](https://www.europarl.europa.eu/doceo/document/E-9-2022-002329_EN.html?utm_source=openai))
  - During the 2021 Kabul airlift, Spain evacuated and temporarily hosted thousands of at‑risk Afghans, creating an EU logistics hub—praised by EU leaders—before distributing evacuees across the EU. ([english.elpais.com](https://english.elpais.com/international/2021-08-23/hundreds-of-afghans-evacuated-to-spanish-air-base-in-operation-lauded-by-eu-leaders.html?utm_source=openai))

- Ongoing commitments against gender violence:
  - In 2025 Sánchez committed to renewing and strengthening Spain’s cross‑party State Pact against Gender‑Based Violence (400 new measures, including protection of minors and tackling trafficking). ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/paginas/2025/20250210-gender-violence-law-anniversary.aspx?utm_source=openai))

Limits and balance: Human-rights practice is complex, and NGOs have documented serious concerns—most notably the 24 June 2022 Melilla tragedy, where at least 37 people died and Amnesty International criticized Spain and Morocco for inadequate investigations. Acknowledging this, the pattern of legislation and international action above is nonetheless strong, sustained, and system‑level evidence that Sánchez prioritizes human rights in policy. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2022/12/morocco-spain-stalled-and-inadequate-inquiries-smack-of-cover-up-six-months-after-37-deaths-at-melilla-border/?utm_source=openai))


2) Sources considered TRUSTWORTHY (and used)

- Source: Boletín Oficial del Estado (via Spanish government repositories such as Norm@doc/BOE)
  - Reasoning 1: Primary, official gazette of Spain; definitive record of enacted laws. 
  - Reasoning 2: Directly relevant to demonstrating human‑rights legislation enacted under Sánchez (e.g., Laws 15/2022, 20/2022, 4/2023, 3/2021). ([hacienda.gob.es](https://www.hacienda.gob.es/DocLeyes/REPOSITORIO%20NORMATIVA/79015.htm?utm_source=openai))

- Source: La Moncloa (Official website of the Government/Prime Minister of Spain)
  - Reasoning 1: Primary executive source; official statements and policy decisions.
  - Reasoning 2: Provides authoritative records of recognition of Palestine, disability‑rights constitutional reform remarks, and GBV‑pact commitments. ([lamoncloa.gob.es](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/lang/en/presidente/news/Paginas/2024/20240528-declaration-palestine.aspx?utm_source=openai))

- Source: United Nations (UNGA press, official HRC election pages)
  - Reasoning 1: Primary, intergovernmental source with high standards of record‑keeping.
  - Reasoning 2: Confirms Spain’s election to the Human Rights Council for 2025–2027. ([un.org](https://www.un.org/en/ga/79/meetings/elections/hrc.shtml?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Spanish Ministry of Foreign Affairs (exteriores.gob.es)
  - Reasoning 1: Official ministerial communications.
  - Reasoning 2: Documents Spain’s HRC membership and human‑rights statements in Geneva. ([exteriores.gob.es](https://www.exteriores.gob.es/en/Comunicacion/NotasPrensa/Paginas/2024_NOTAS_P/20241009_NOTA062.aspx?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Venice Commission, Council of Europe
  - Reasoning 1: Authoritative constitutional law advisory body of the Council of Europe.
  - Reasoning 2: Its opinions clarify the rule‑of‑law parameters around Spain’s amnesty law (reconciliation as legitimate aim; recommendations on scope/consensus). ([coe.int](https://www.coe.int/en/web/venice-commission/-/cdl-ad-2024-003-e?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Associated Press (AP)
  - Reasoning 1: Global wire service with rigorous editorial standards.
  - Reasoning 2: Used for independent confirmation of major institutional changes (e.g., Article 49 constitutional amendment) and earlier statements on Palestine recognition plans. ([apnews.com](https://apnews.com/article/c0d4d2420118232eb04996b106f01289?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Reuters
  - Reasoning 1: Reputable international news agency; strong fact‑checking.
  - Reasoning 2: Used to corroborate court rulings and policy follow‑through (e.g., Constitutional Court decision upholding the amnesty law; Spain’s Afghanistan operations context). ([reuters.com](https://www.reuters.com/world/spains-top-court-upholds-amnesty-law-catalan-separatists-2025-06-26/?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Deutsche Welle (DW)
  - Reasoning 1: German public broadcaster; reliable international reporting.
  - Reasoning 2: Independent coverage of amnesty law milestones and context. ([dw.com](https://www.dw.com/en/spain-gives-final-approval-to-catalan-amnesty-bill/a-69222400?utm_source=openai))

- Source: RTVE (Spain’s public broadcaster)
  - Reasoning 1: Public‑service media with editorial oversight.
  - Reasoning 2: Summarizes Venice Commission’s conclusions about the amnesty law’s legitimacy conditions and process caveats. ([rtve.es](https://www.rtve.es/noticias/20240315/comision-venecia-dictamen-final-ley-amnistia/16016754.shtml?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Cornell Law School Legal Information Institute (LII)
  - Reasoning 1: Academic legal repository; accurate summaries with citations.
  - Reasoning 2: Clear, cited overview of Spain’s 2023 LGBTI/trans law features (self‑ID, minors’ safeguards, conversion‑therapy ban). ([law.cornell.edu](https://www.law.cornell.edu/gender-justice/resource/Ley_4/2023_de_28_de_febrero_de_2023_para_la_igualdad_real_y_efectiva_de_las_personas_trans_y_para_la_garant%C3%ADa_de_los_derechos_de_las_personas_LGTBI_on_equality_of_trans_and_LGBTI_persons?utm_source=openai))

- Source: El País (English edition)
  - Reasoning 1: Major Spanish newspaper of record.
  - Reasoning 2: Used narrowly to document EU leaders’ praise of Spain’s Afghan evacuation hub. ([english.elpais.com](https://english.elpais.com/international/2021-08-23/hundreds-of-afghans-evacuated-to-spanish-air-base-in-operation-lauded-by-eu-leaders.html?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Amnesty International
  - Reasoning 1: Leading global human‑rights NGO with documented methodologies.
  - Reasoning 2: Provides credible counter‑evidence on Melilla, useful for balanced assessment. ([amnesty.org](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2022/12/morocco-spain-stalled-and-inadequate-inquiries-smack-of-cover-up-six-months-after-37-deaths-at-melilla-border/?utm_source=openai))

- Source: Wikipedia (various pages)
  - Reasoning 1: Open‑edit encyclopedia; quality varies; not a primary source.
  - Reasoning 2: Acceptable for orientation but not for proof of government actions; therefore not used for core claims here. (We verified with primary/official sources instead.) ([en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ley_del_solo_s%C3%AD_es_s%C3%AD?utm_source=openai))
